People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me. Um, it's really nice to meet you. 

I guess I'm here because... well, I've been doing some things lately that don't really make a lot of sense. Just at work and school mostly. I'm a bookkeeping clerk, and I really try to be a good employee, I promise, but lately I've been getting into these little habits that I know probably look a bit strange to other people. 

Like, I'll start lining up all the receipts in a big line across the floor. Or I'll catch myself just tapping the calculator, always in sets of nine. It's honestly a little embarrassing to talk about, because I know it sounds weird. The other day, I was just standing by the copier with my hands held up in the air, and I couldn't really put them down until someone walked by and asked if I was okay. 

I'm sure it's just stress or something minor, right? I hope it's okay that I came in to talk to you about it. Is that the sort of thing you can help me with?

The other day, I was just standing by the copier with my hands held up in the air, and I couldn't really put them down until someone walked by and asked if I was okay. \n\nI'm sure it's just stress or something minor, right? I hope it's okay that I came in to talk to you about it. Is that the sort of thing you can help me with?\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're doing these things—like lining up the receipts or tapping the calculator in sets of nine—do you feel like you want to stop, or do you feel driven to keep doing them even though you know they might seem odd to others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 22 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, they're definitely more... complex, I think. Like, they look like they have a purpose, maybe? It's not just like my finger twitching on a button or something simple like that. 

Like when I line the receipts up across the floor, I'm, you know, placing them down in a specific way. Or when I'm tapping the calculator, it's specifically in sets of nine. And holding my hands up by the copier... it's a whole posture, you know? I just stay like that until someone comes by and asks if I'm okay. 

It's not just random jerking around. It looks like I'm doing a task, even though I know it doesn't make any sense to anyone else. Um, no, not really like a strict rule or anything. I mean, I guess I said sets of nine before, but... it's not like I'm counting them out perfectly every single time, or like something bad will happen if it's only eight taps, you know?

It's just sort of... how it happens when I space out. I don't feel forced to do it exactly one way. I could probably do it differently if I really thought about it, but like I said, I'm just not really thinking about it when it happens. \n\nI'm not, like, crazy strict about it having to be perfect. I know that would probably sound pretty bad, right? I just want to make sure you know I'm not stuck in some weird set of rules. I'm really trying to be flexible and normal. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well... I guess they look like they might have a purpose, maybe? Like, to someone just glancing over, it might look like I'm doing real clerk work. 

When I'm lining the receipts across the floor, I mean, I'm organizing them, right? Just... not in a way that actually helps with the bookkeeping. And tapping the calculator, it looks like I'm doing math, even if I'm just doing those sets of nine or whatever. And standing by the copier with my hands up... well, I guess that one is a little harder to explain away. I just kind of stay there until someone asks if I'm okay. 

But I'm really not trying to be disruptive or anything. I know it doesn't make sense, and that's why I'm here. I want to be doing actual, useful work. I don't want people to think I'm just doing pointless, crazy things. It's better if it looks like I'm at least trying to accomplish something, right? I'm really hoping we can fix this so I can just be a normal, productive worker again.
